{"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_5","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,260p' src/index.js\"","aggregated_output":"/**\n * Routines for converting binary data into text data which can be sent safely\n * through 'Unicode-clean' text systems without information being lost. Analogous\n * to Base64 with a significantly larger character repertoire enabling the\n * encoding of 2.00 bytes per character (for comparison, Base64 manages 0.75 bytes\n * per character).\n */\n\n'use strict'\n\n// Z is a number, usually a uint16 but sometimes a uint8\n\nconst BITS_PER_CHAR = 16 // Base65536 is an 16-bit encoding\nconst BITS_PER_BYTE = 8\n\n// Compressed representation of inclusive-exclusive ranges of characters used in this encoding.\nconst pairStrings = [\n  '㐀䳿一黿ꄀꏿꔀꗿ𐘀�𒀀�𓀀�𔐀�𖠀�𠀀�',\n  'ᔀᗿ'\n]\n\n// Decompression\nconst lookupE = {}\nconst lookupD = {}\npairStrings.forEach((pairString, r) => {\n  const numZBits = BITS_PER_CHAR - BITS_PER_BYTE * r // 0 -> 16, 1 -> 8\n  lookupE[numZBits] = {}\n  let z2 = 0\n  pairString.match(/../gu).forEach(pair => {\n    const [first, last] = [...pair].map(x => x.codePointAt(0))\n    for (let codePoint = first; codePoint <= last; codePoint++) {\n      const chr = String.fromCodePoint(codePoint)\n\n      // SPECIAL CASE: flip the bytes around, because Base65536 was constructed to take the bytes\n      // in the wrong order originally\n      const z = numZBits === BITS_PER_CHAR ? 256 * (z2 % 256) + (z2 >> 8) : z2\n\n      lookupE[numZBits][z] = chr\n      lookupD[chr] = [numZBits, z]\n      z2++\n    }\n  })\n})\n\nconst encode = uint8Array => {\n  const length = uint8Array.length\n\n  let str = ''\n  let z = 0\n  let numZBits = 0\n\n  for (let i = 0; i < length; i++) {\n    const uint8 = uint8Array[i]\n\n    // Take most significant bit first\n    for (let j = BITS_PER_BYTE - 1; j >= 0; j--) {\n      const bit = (uint8 >> j) & 1\n\n      z = (z << 1) + bit\n      numZBits++\n\n      if (numZBits === BITS_PER_CHAR) {\n        str += lookupE[numZBits][z]\n        z = 0\n        numZBits = 0\n      }\n    }\n  }\n\n  if (numZBits !== 0) {\n    // Final bits require special treatment.\n    while (!(numZBits in lookupE)) {\n      z = (z << 1) + 1\n      numZBits++\n    }\n\n    str += lookupE[numZBits][z]\n  }\n\n  return str\n}\n\nconst decode = str => {\n  const length = str.length\n\n  // This length is a guess. There's a chance we allocate one more byte here\n  // than we actually need. But we can count and slice it off later\n  const uint8Array = new Uint8Array(Math.floor(length * BITS_PER_CHAR / BITS_PER_BYTE))\n  let numUint8s = 0\n  let uint8 = 0\n  let numUint8Bits = 0\n  let shouldBeNoMoreChars = false\n\n  for (const chr of str) {\n    if (shouldBeNoMoreChars) {\n      throw new Error('Secondary character found before end of input')\n    }\n\n    if (!(chr in lookupD)) {\n      throw new Error(`Unrecognised Base65536 character: ${chr}`)\n    }\n\n    const [numZBits, z] = lookupD[chr]\n\n    // Take most significant bit first\n    for (let j = numZBits - 1; j >= 0; j--) {\n      const bit = (z >> j) & 1\n\n      uint8 = (uint8 << 1) + bit\n      numUint8Bits++\n\n      if (numUint8Bits === BITS_PER_BYTE) {\n        uint8Array[numUint8s] = uint8\n        numUint8s++\n        uint8 = 0\n        numUint8Bits = 0\n      }\n    }\n\n    if (numZBits !== BITS_PER_CHAR) {\n      shouldBeNoMoreChars = true\n    }\n  }\n\n  // Final padding bits! Requires special consideration!\n  // Remember how we always pad with 1s?\n  // Note: there could be 0 such bits, check still works though\n  if (uint8 !== ((1 << numUint8Bits) - 1)) {\n    throw new Error('Padding mismatch')\n  }\n\n  return new Uint8Array(uint8Array.buffer, 0, numUint8s)\n}\n\nexport { encode, decode }\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

(For transmitting data through Twitter, Base65536 is now considered obsolete; see [Base2048](https://github.com/qntm/base2048).) This JavaScript module, `base65536`, is the first implementation of this encoding.\n./README.md:5:Base65536 uses only [\"safe\" Unicode code points](https://qntm.org/safe) - no unassigned code points, no whitespace, no control characters, *etc.*.\n./README.md:25:      <td>Unary / <a href=\"https://github.com/qntm/base1\">Base1</a></td>\n./README.md:61:      <td><a href=\"https://github.com/qntm/hexagram-encode\">HexagramEncode</a></td>\n./README.md:68:      <td><a href=\"https://github.com/qntm/braille-encode\">BrailleEncode</a></td>\n./README.md:75:      <td><a href=\"https://github.com/qntm/base2048\">Base2048</a></td>\n./README.md:82:      <td><a href=\"https://github.com/qntm/base32768\">Base32768</a></td>\n./README.md:90:      <td><a href=\"https://github.com/keith-turner/ecoji\">Ecoji</a></td>\n./README.md:97:      <td><a href=\"https://github.com/qntm/base65536\">Base65536</a></td>\n./README.md:104:      <td><a href=\"https://github.com/qntm/base131072\">Base131072</a> ‡</td>\n./README.md:149:Encodes a [`Uint8Array`](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/JavaScript/Reference/Global_Objects/Uint8Array) and returns a Base65536 `String`, suitable for passing safely through almost any \"Unicode-clean\" text-handling API. This string contains no special characters and is immune to Unicode normalization. The string encodes two bytes per code point.\n./README.md:161:I wanted people to be able to share [HATETRIS](http://qntm.org/files/hatetris/hatetris.html) replays via Twitter.\n./README.md:182:However, interestingly, \"[Tweet length is measured by the number of codepoints in the NFC normalized version of the text.](https://dev.twitter.com/overview/api/counting-characters)\", *not* by counting the number of bytes in any specific encoding of the text. Realising this, I developed Base65536. Whereas Base64 encodes only 6 bits per Unicode code point for a potential maximum of 105 bytes per Tweet, Base65536 encodes 16 bits per Unicode code point for a vastly improved **280 bytes per Tweet**.\n./README.md:188:This [fit comfortably in a Tweet](https://twitter.com/qntm/status/673523018224791552), with an extravagant 12 characters left over for commentary. Of course, worse HATETRIS players typically end up with shorter replays, leaving more room for invective.\n./README.md:198:Later in 2017, Twitter <a href=\"https://blog.twitter.com/official/en_us/topics/product/2017/Giving-you-more-characters-to-express-yourself.html\">increased the maximum Tweet length</a> from 140 to 280 Unicode code points... except that code points U+1100 HANGUL CHOSEONG KIYEOK upwards now counted double. This effectively divided Unicode into \"light\" code points and \"heavy\" code points. Since Base65536 exclusively uses heavy code points, a new \"long\" Tweet could still only contain at most 140 code points of Base65536, or 280 bytes.\n./README.md:200:These two events spurred me to develop a new binary encoding optimised for the new long Tweets, [Base2048](https://github.com/qntm/base2048). Using Base2048, up to 385 bytes can fit in a Tweet. The previously unTweetable record 31-point replay becomes:\n./README.md:204:which, yes, [fits just fine](https://twitter.com/qntm/status/931634672236449793).\n./README.md:214:To encode one additional bit per code point, we need to *double* the number of code points we use from 65,536 to 131,072. This would be a new encoding, [Base131072](https://github.com/qntm/base131072), and its UTF-32 encoding efficiency would be 53% vs. 50% for Base65536. (Note that in UTF-16, [Base32768](https://github.com/qntm/base32768) significantly outperforms either choice, and in UTF-8, Base64 remains the preferred choice.)\n./README.md:216:However, as of Unicode 10.0, [`safe-code-point`](https://github.com/qntm/safe-code-point) returns only 116,813 safe code points altogether. Perhaps future versions of Unicode will eventually assign more characters and make this possible, but even when this eventually happens, it seems unlikely that the characters will be arranged neatly in the blocks of 256 which make Base65536 so small and simple. {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_16","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,280p' scripts/gen.js\"","aggregated_output":"// This module's purpose is to generate 65,792 \"safe\" <https://qntm.org/safe>\n// Unicode code points suitable for use in the Base65536 encoding. It makes use\n// of the sibling package `safe-code-point`\n// <https://github.com/qntm/safe-code-point>.\n\n// 65,792 is 2**16 + 2**8. Each code point in the initial collection of 2**16\n// code points encodes 16 bits, i.e., a distinct possible pair of bytes. The\n// extra 2**8 code points are needed to encode 8 bits of the single final byte\n// in cases where the binary data runs for an odd number of bytes.\n\n// Rather than generate thousands and thousands of arbitrary safe code points,\n// this module simplifies matters by finding 256 + 1 contiguous, aligned blocks\n// of 256 safe code points. This means the last 8 bits of the code point can be\n// decoded directly to an encoded byte, and the lookup table to decode the other\n// byte (if any) is relatively small.\n\n// This program was run only once, with the successful results immediately\n// transplanted into `base65536` for use. It is kept here for historical reasons\n// and to ensure reproducibility.\n\nimport SafeCodePoint from 'safe-code-point'\n\n// Well these ergonomics are dreadful huh\nconst safeCodePoint = await SafeCodePoint('8.0.0')\n\nconst safeRange = (min, max) => {\n  for (let codePoint = min; codePoint < max; codePoint++) {\n    // Code points were chosen entirely from the \"Letter, other\" general\n    // category, for reasons which I no longer recall. Unicode 8.0 was current\n    // at the time.\n    if (\n      safeCodePoint.generalCategory(codePoint) !== 'Lo' ||\n      !safeCodePoint(codePoint)\n    ) {\n      return false\n    }\n  }\n  return true\n}\n\nconst getAllSafeRanges = rangeSize => {\n  const allSafeRanges = []\n  for (let codePoint = 0; codePoint < (1 << 16) + (1 << 20); codePoint += rangeSize) {\n    if (safeRange(codePoint, codePoint + rangeSize)) {\n      allSafeRanges.push(codePoint)\n    }\n  }\n  return allSafeRanges\n}\n\nconst allSafeRanges = getAllSafeRanges(1 << 8)\n\nconst paddingBlockStart = String.fromCodePoint(allSafeRanges.shift())\n\nconst blockStarts = allSafeRanges.slice(0, 1 << 8).map(x => String.fromCodePoint(x)).join('')\n\nexport { safeCodePoint, paddingBlockStart, blockStarts }\n\n// There are now implementations of\n// Base65536 in numerous programming languages beyond the original JavaScript,\n// and I consider it *extremely* undesirable to introduce multiple incompatible\n// versions of the Base65536 encoding, so this program is unlikely to ever be\n// run again unless a very serious problem is discovered.\n\n// Still, if I tried this again, here are some things I might do differently:\n\n// * Include other safe Unicode General Categories, so that Base65536 output\n// does not seemingly consist only of CJK characters. At present, to my eye (not\n// being a reader of those languages) it resembles ordinary CJK text. I would\n// prefer Base65536 to be more obviously a kludge of diverse scripts, so that it\n// can't be mistaken for text, even by someone who knows none of those scripts.\n// Naturally the lion's share of code points will have to remain CJK though,\n// that's just where the safe code points mostly are.\n// * Try harder to select lower code points, those with 3-byte encodings in\n// UTF-8 and 2-byte encodings in UTF-16, to reduce the average size of the\n// encoded Base65536 output (even though Base65536 is optimised for UTF-32).\n// * Perhaps try to find larger blocks of 512 or 1024 code points rather than\n// 256, or other techniques for reducing the size of the lookup tables.\n// * Choose all characters with the same East_Asian_Width of 'W' (wide).\n// * Make Base65536 big-endian, so that [0x00 0x00] and [0x00 0x01] encode to\n// code points which are separated by 1 - this would simplify decoding and be\n// more consistent with my other encodings.\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}
